We also note the following symmetry properties of these coordinates:

Proposition A.1
.

The expansions
u_{j}(z)
and
u_{j}(-z)
,
j=1,2,3,4
are related by

\displaystyle u_{1}(-z)=-u_{1}(z),\qquad

\displaystyle u_{2}(-z)=-u_{2}(z),

\displaystyle u_{3}(-z)=-u_{4}(z),\qquad

\displaystyle u_{4}(-z)=-u_{3}(z).

Proof.

This proposition can be observed directly from the expansions of
u_{j}(z)
given above.
∎

Appendix B
The isomonodromic tau function

The partition function of the
2
-matrix model was identified with an isomonodromic
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-function by Bertola and Marchal,
cf.
[
9
]
. Their derivation is relatively straightforward, and applies almost directly to our situation. However, some of
the details of the calculation are different enough that the proof merits discussion. We present the proof of the fact
that the partition function for the
2
-matrix model is an isomonodromic
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-function here. The proof mirrors almost directly that of
Bertola and Marchal’s; one should consult their work and references therein for further details and commentary.
For sake of readability, let us introduce the notation, for a given matrix-valued
1
-form
A(z)
,

\langle A(z)\rangle:=\displaystyle\Ress_{z=\infty}\tr[A(z)].

Recall that, in general, the
n^{\rm th}
monic biorthogonal polynomial
p_{n}(z)
with respect to the weight
8
8

8

Here we take
N>0
as a free parameter, in general different from the index of the biorthogonal polynomial. We will later set
N=n
.

{\rm e}^{NW(z,w)}:={\rm e}^{N[\tau zw-V(z;T)-V(w;\bar{T})]}

(
Here
V(z;T):=\frac{1}{2}z^{2}+\frac{T}{4}z^{4}
, and we take
T
,
\bar{T}
as independent parameters in general
)
on contour(s)
(\Gamma,\Gamma)

is given in terms of the solution to the following Riemann–Hilbert problem:

(1)

\YY_{n}(z)
is a piecewise analytic function in
\CC\setminus\Gamma
.

(2)

\YY_{n}(z)
has boundary values

\displaystyle\YY_{n,+}(z)=\YY_{n,-}(z){\rm e}^{-NV(z)}\!\biggl[\mathbb{I}+w(z)E_{12}+\frac{1}{N\tau}w^{\prime}(z)E_{13}+\frac{1}{N^{2}\tau^{2}}w^{\prime\prime}(z)E_{14}\biggr],\quad z\in\Gamma,

where
w(z):=\int_{\Gamma}{\rm e}^{N[\tau zw-V(w,\bar{T})]}{\rm d}w
(note that in the general situation, the integration contour
\Gamma
is allowed to differ from the jump contour
\Gamma
; in this work, this distinction is irrelevant).

(3)

As
z\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\YY_{n}(z)=\biggl[\mathbb{I}+\frac{\YY_{n}^{(1)}}{z}+\frac{\YY_{n}^{(2)}}{z^{2}}+\OO\biggl(\frac{1}{z^{3}}\biggr)\biggr]\begin{pmatrix}z^{n}&0&0\\
0&z^{-m_{n}-1}\mathbb{I}_{r_{n}}&0\\
0&0&z^{-m_{n}}\mathbb{I}_{3-r_{n}}\\
\end{pmatrix},

where
m_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
,
r_{n}\in\{0,1,2\}
are such that
n=3m_{n}+r_{n}
.

Note that, when
n=3k
is a multiple of
3
, the above coincides with the Riemann Hilbert problem analyzed in the present work.
If we put
h_{n}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T};N\bigr)
to be the norming constant for the monic
n^{\rm th}
-order polynomials,

\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\Gamma}p_{n}(z)q_{m}(w){\rm e}^{NW(z,w)}{\rm d}z{\rm d}w=h_{n}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T};N\bigr)\delta_{nm},

then the
partition function
of this model is defined to be

Z_{n}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T};N\bigr):=\prod_{k=0}^{n-1}h_{n}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T};N\bigr).

We now summarize some of the basic results pertaining to this RHP which we will need in our analysis of the
\tau
-differential.

The solution to this Riemann–Hilbert problem is given explicitly,

\YY_{n}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}p_{n}(z)&C_{\Gamma}[p_{n}w](z)&\frac{1}{N\tau}C_{\Gamma}[p_{n}w^{\prime}](z)&\frac{1}{N^{2}\tau^{2}}C_{\Gamma}[p_{n}w^{\prime\prime}](z)\\[2.56073pt]
Q_{n-1}(z)&C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-1}w](z)&\frac{1}{N\tau}C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-1}w^{\prime}](z)&\frac{1}{N^{2}\tau^{2}}C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-1}w^{\prime\prime}](z)\\[2.56073pt]
Q_{n-2}(z)&C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-2}w](z)&\frac{1}{N\tau}C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-2}w^{\prime}](z)&\frac{1}{N^{2}\tau^{2}}C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-2}w^{\prime\prime}](z)\\[2.56073pt]
Q_{n-3}(z)&C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-3}w](z)&\frac{1}{N\tau}C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-3}w^{\prime}](z)&\frac{1}{N^{2}\tau^{2}}C_{\Gamma}[Q_{n-3}w^{\prime\prime}](z)\end{pmatrix},

where
Q_{n-1}
,
Q_{n-2}
, and
Q_{n-3}
are some appropriately chosen polynomials of degrees
n-1
,
n-2
, and
n-3
, respectively,
p_{n}(z)
is the

n^{\rm th}
monic biorthogonal polynomial, and

C_{\Gamma}[f](z):=\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\int_{\Gamma}\frac{f(x)}{x-z}{\rm d}z

denotes the Cauchy transform with respect to the contour
\Gamma
. We can also relate the Riemann–Hilbert problem for
\YY_{n}(z)
to the Riemann–Hilbert
problem for
\YY_{n+1}(z)
by means of a
raising operator

\YY_{n+1}(z)=R_{n}(z)\YY_{n}(z),

where
R_{n}(z):=R^{(1)}_{n}z+R^{(0)}_{n}
is a degree
1
matrix-valued polynomial in
z
. The existence of
R_{n}(z)
follows immediately from the fact that

\YY_{n+1}(z)\YY_{n}^{-1}(z)
has no jumps, and thus extends to an entire function. The asymptotics of
\YY_{n+1}(z)\YY_{n}^{-1}(z)
uniquely fix the form of

R_{n}(z)
. Setting
\alpha_{0}:=r_{N}+1
, we have that
R^{(1)}_{n}=E_{11}
, whereas the matrix
\bigl(R^{(0)}_{n}\bigr)_{jk}
has entries as given in the following table:

k=\alpha_{0}

k=1

k\neq 1,\alpha_{0}

j=\alpha_{0}

\frac{-(\YY_{n}^{(2)})_{\alpha_{0},1}+\sum_{\ell\neq\alpha_{0}}(\YY_{n}^{(1)})_{\alpha_{0},\ell}\bigl(\YY_{n}^{(1)}\bigr)_{\ell,1}}{(\YY_{n,1})_{\alpha_{0},1}}

-\bigl(\YY_{n}^{(1)}\bigr)_{\alpha_{0},1}

-\bigl(\YY_{n}^{(1)}\bigr)_{\alpha_{0},k}

j=1

\frac{1}{(\YY_{n}^{(1)})_{\alpha_{0},1}}

0

0

j\neq 1,\alpha_{0}

\frac{(\YY_{n}^{(1)})_{j,1}}{(\YY_{n}^{(1)})_{\alpha_{0},1}}

0

\delta_{jk}

This implies that the matrix
R_{n}(z)
is determined entirely in terms of
\YY_{n}(z)
.

The isomonodromic
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-differential corresponding to
\YY:=\YY_{n}
is defined to be

{\rm d}\log\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}:=\bigl\langle\YY_{n}^{-1}\YY_{n}^{\prime}{\rm d}\hat{\WW}\hat{\WW}^{-1}\bigr\rangle,

where
\hat{\WW}(z)
is essentially the augmented
\WW
-matrix from the first transformation

\hat{\WW}(z):=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm e}^{-NV(z)}&0\\
0&\WW^{-1}(z)\end{pmatrix},

with the only difference here being that
V(z)=\frac{1}{2}z^{2}+\frac{\bar{T}}{4}z^{4}
. For now, we treat the parameter
N
in the matrix
\hat{\WW}
as a
fixed
parameter independent of the index of the polynomial
n
; we shall later set
N=n
. Since multiplication by
\hat{\WW}
yields a constant jump RHP, we have the following formulae for the differential of
{\rm d}\hat{\WW}\hat{\WW}^{-1}
:
renders
\YY

{\rm d}\hat{\WW}\hat{\WW}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&-\WW^{-1}\frac{\partial\WW}{\partial\tau}\end{pmatrix}{\rm d}\tau+\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&-\WW^{-1}\frac{\partial\WW}{\partial T}\end{pmatrix}{\rm d}T+\begin{pmatrix}-Nz^{4}/4&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}{\rm d}\bar{T}.

In
[
9
]
, the parameters of the isomonodromic
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-differential come from the coefficients of the potential (as opposed to
our case, where one of the parameters is the coefficient of the interaction term
XY
; namely,
\tau
), and the
definition of
{\rm d}\hat{\WW}\hat{\WW}^{-1}
is slightly different. However, it is only important to the proof that
\hat{\WW}
renders the jumps of
\YY
constant, which we have already seen (indeed, this was the point of the first transformation
\YY\mapsto\XX
).

The biorthogonal polynomials double as a particular sequence of multiple orthogonal polynomials. Summarizing the arguments of
[
9
]
, one can use the sequence of raising operators arising from the multiple orthogonality to produce the next
biorthogonal polynomial
in the sequence. Let us
denote this raising operator generically by
R_{n}(z)
.
R_{n}(z)
is defined so that

\YY_{n+1}(z)=R_{n}(z)\YY_{n}(z).

(B.1)

Generically,
R_{n}(z)
is a degree
1
polynomial in
z
; its inverse is also a degree
1
polynomial in
z
. We have the following proposition.

Proposition B.1
.

{\rm d}\log\frac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n+1}}{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}}=-\bigl\langle R_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{\prime}{\rm d}\YY_{n}\YY^{-1}_{n}\bigr\rangle.

Proof.

By equation (
B.1
), we have that

\YY_{n+1}^{-1}\YY_{n+1}^{\prime}=\YY_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{\prime}\YY_{n}+\YY_{n}^{-1}\YY_{n}^{\prime}.

Thus, the quotient of
\boldsymbol{\tau}
differentials is

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\rm d}\log\frac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n+1}}{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}}&=\bigl\langle\bigl[\YY_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{\prime}\YY_{n}+\YY_{n}^{-1}\YY_{n}^{\prime}\bigr]{\rm d}\hat{\WW}\hat{\WW}^{-1}\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle\YY_{n}^{-1}\YY_{n}^{\prime}{\rm d}\hat{\WW}\hat{\WW}^{-1}\bigr\rangle\\
&=\bigl\langle\YY_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{\prime}\YY_{n}{\rm d}\hat{\WW}\hat{\WW}^{-1}\bigr\rangle.\end{split}

Now, recall that
\YY_{n}\WW
has constant jumps, and so the differential
{\rm d}[\YY_{n}\WW]\WW^{-1}\YY_{n}^{-1}
has coefficients which
are polynomial in
z
, by the standard Liouville argument. This statement can be rewritten as

\YY_{n}{\rm d}\hat{\WW}\hat{\WW}^{-1}\YY_{n}^{-1}={\rm d}\YY_{n}\YY_{n}^{-1}+\text{polynomial}.

Inserting the above into our expression for the
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-quotient, we obtain that

{\rm d}\log\frac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n+1}}{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}}=\bigl\langle R_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{\prime}[\text{polynomial}]-R_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{\prime}{\rm d}\YY_{n}\YY_{n}^{-1}\bigr\rangle;

since
R_{n}^{-1}R_{n}^{\prime}
is a polynomial in
z
, the first term is a polynomial in
z
, and thus has no residues at infinity. This completes the proof.
∎

We can use the explicit form of the raising operators obtained before to get an expression for the
\tau
-differential in terms of the coefficients of

\YY_{n}(z)
. The exact expression is summarized by the following proposition.

Proposition B.2
.

The ratio of consecutive
\boldsymbol{\tau}
differentials, up to multiplication by a function independent of the isomonodromic times, is given by

\frac{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n+1}}{\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}}=\bigl(\YY_{n}^{(1)}\bigr)_{1,\alpha_{0}}.

Proof.

This follows immediately from inspection of the previous proposition, and the explicit form of the matrices
R_{n}(z)
. For details, see
[
9
]
.
∎

Furthermore, we can relate the coefficients
\bigl(\YY_{n}^{(1)}\bigr)_{1,\alpha_{0}}
to the biorthogonality coefficients
h_{n}
.

Proposition B.3
.

The matrix coefficient
\bigl(\YY_{n}^{(1)}\bigr)_{1,\alpha_{0}}
is given in terms of the
n^{\rm th}
normalizing constant of the biorthogonal polynomials:

\bigl(\YY_{n}^{(1)}\bigr)_{1,\alpha_{0}}=\biggl(\frac{T}{\tau}\biggr)^{S}h_{n},

where
S\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{0}\in\{0,1,2\}
are such that
n=3S+\alpha_{0}-1
.

Proposition B.4
.

The isomonodromic
\boldsymbol{\tau}
-function
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}
is related to the partition function
Z_{n}(\tau,t,\allowbreak H;N)
for
n
a multiple of
3
by
the formula

Z_{n}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T};N\bigr)=\Bigl(\frac{\tau}{T}\Bigr)^{\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{3}-1)}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}.

Remark B.5
.

We remark that there is a minor error in the statement of the above proposition in
[
9
]
. The power of
(T/\tau)
should be inverse to what it reads in their Theorem 3.4 (see page 17). The proof is otherwise correct, and there are no essential changes to the results otherwise.

Thus, we have an explicit expression for the partition function in terms of quantities we can calculate, by making the change of variables
T=qt
,
\bar{T}=q^{-1}t
. However, the calculation of the differential in the variable
\bar{T}
is computationally difficult, as it would require us to calculate the expansion of
\YY
to 4 subleading terms. However, we can use the fact that the partition function is symmetric in
T
,
\bar{T}
; in other words,

{\rm d}\log Z_{n}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T};N\bigr)={\rm d}\log Z_{n}\bigl(\tau,\bar{T},T;N\bigr).

If we set

\displaystyle\varpi_{\tau}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T},n;N\bigr):=\bigl\langle\YY_{n}^{-1}\YY_{n}^{\prime}\hat{\WW}_{\tau}\hat{\WW}^{-1}\bigr\rangle,\qquad\varpi_{T}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T},n;N\bigr):=\bigl\langle\YY_{n}^{-1}\YY_{n}^{\prime}\hat{\WW}_{T}\hat{\WW}^{-1}\bigr\rangle,

then we have the following proposition, which follows immediately from the symmetry

\log Z_{n}(T,\bar{T})=\log Z_{n}(\bar{T},T),

and our previous calculations.

Proposition B.6
.

The partition function is given by the expression

\displaystyle{\rm d}\log Z_{n}={\rm d}\log\Bigl(\frac{\tau}{T\bar{T}}\Bigr)^{\frac{n}{2}(\frac{n}{3}-1)}+\varpi_{\tau}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T},n;N\bigr){\rm d}\tau+\varpi_{T}\bigl(\tau,T,\bar{T},n;N\bigr){\rm d}T

\displaystyle\hphantom{{\rm d} \log Z_n =}{}+\varpi_{T}\bigl(\tau,\bar{T},T,n;N\bigr){\rm d}\bar{T}.

Appendix C
Explicit formulae for the spectral curve

and the critical surface

Here we present explicit formulae for the spectral curve
\mathfrak{S}(X,Y)=0
, and the critical surface
\Xi(\tau,t,q)=0
. Both of these formulae are rather longer and not particularly enlightening. We have therefore relinquished their presentation in the main text and placed them here, for the sake of completeness.

C.1
Implicit formula for the spectral curve

One can eliminate the parameter
u
from the pair of functions
(X(u),Y(u))
to obtain an implicit formula for the Riemann surface these functions parametrize

\mathfrak{S}(X(u),Y(u))=0.

\mathfrak{S}(X,Y)
is a degree
6
polynomial in
X
and
Y
, with rational coefficients in the variables
\tau
,
t
,
q
, and
\sigma
(recall that
\sigma
is defined as a special solution to the algebraic equation (
2.1
)). Explicitly, this polynomial is

\displaystyle\mathfrak{S}(X,Y)={}

\displaystyle\tau qX^{4}+\tau q^{-1}Y^{4}-tX^{3}Y^{3}-qX^{3}Y-q^{-1}Y^{3}X+t\tau^{-1}X^{2}Y^{2}

\displaystyle{}{+}\,\mathfrak{s}_{2}(\sigma;\tau,t,q)X^{2}+\mathfrak{s}_{2}\bigl(\sigma;\tau,t,q^{-1}\bigr)Y^{2}+\mathfrak{s}_{1}(\sigma;\tau,t,q)XY+\mathfrak{s}_{0}(\sigma;\tau,t,q).

where
\mathfrak{s}_{i}(\sigma;\tau,t,q)
are given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{2}(\sigma;\tau,t,q)=\frac{1-6\sigma-3\sigma^{2}}{27\tau t(\sigma+1)^{3}}-\frac{1}{27\tau t}\bigl(\sigma^{3}\tau^{2}+3\sigma^{2}\tau^{2}-9\sigma\tau^{2}-27\tau^{2}+1\bigr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathfrak{s}_2(\sigma;\tau,t,q) =}{}-\frac{(q-1)\sigma}{27\tau t(\sigma^{2}-1)^{3}}\bigl[\bigl(9\tau^{2}-9\bigr)+9(q+1)\sigma+\bigl(4q^{-1}-\bigl(28\tau^{2}+6\bigr)\bigr)\sigma^{2}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathfrak{s}_2(\sigma;\tau,t,q) =}\qquad{}-6(q+1)\sigma^{3}+\bigl(30\tau^{2}+3\bigr)\sigma^{4}+(q+1)\sigma^{5}-12\sigma^{6}\tau^{2}+\sigma^{8}\tau^{2}\bigr],

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{1}(\sigma;\tau,t,q)=-\frac{8\bigl(5\sigma+3\bigr)}{81(\sigma+1)^{2}t}-\frac{\sigma^{6}\tau^{2}-15\sigma^{4}\tau^{2}+27\sigma^{2}\tau^{2}-3\sigma^{2}+243\tau^{2}+24\sigma+171}{243t}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathfrak{s}_1(\sigma;\tau,t,q) =}{}-\frac{4\sigma^{3}\bigl(\sigma^{2}+3\bigr)}{81t(\sigma^{2}-1)^{2}}\bigl[q+q^{-1}-2\bigr],

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}_{0}(\sigma;\tau,t,q)=-\frac{\sigma}{19683t^{2}\tau q^{2}(\sigma^{2}-1)^{4}}\biggl[15q\sigma^{6}\tau^{2}+9q\sigma^{5}t+\bigl(12-111\tau^{2}\bigr)q\sigma^{4}

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathfrak{s}_0(\sigma;\tau,t,q) =}\qquad{}+15\biggl(q^{2}-\frac{6}{5}tq+1\biggr)\sigma^{3}+\bigl(177\tau^{2}-15\bigr)q\sigma^{2}-54\biggl(q^{2}-\frac{1}{6}tq+1\biggr)\sigma

\displaystyle\hphantom{\mathfrak{s}_0(\sigma;\tau,t,q) =}\qquad{}+81\bigl(1-\tau^{2}\bigr)q\biggr]^{2}.

C.2
Implicit formula for the critical surface

Recall that the critical surface is characterized by the vanishing of the discriminant of the polynomial
\mathfrak{I}(\sigma;\tau,t,q)
, defined in
equation (
2.1
). In other words, let
\Xi(\tau,t,q)
denote this discriminant. Then, we have that

(\tau,t,q)\in S_{L}\cup S_{H}\ \Longrightarrow\ \Xi(\tau,t,q)=0.

Explicitly, we can write this curve as
\bigl(C=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(q+q^{-1}\bigr)=\cosh(H)\bigr)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\tau,t,C):={}

\displaystyle 32400000t\tau^{4}C^{5}+1350000\tau^{4}\bigl(\tau^{4}+288t^{2}-2\tau^{2}+1\bigr)C^{4}

\displaystyle{}{+}\,36000t\tau^{2}\bigl(3160\tau^{6}+28026t^{2}\tau^{2}-2745\tau^{4}+4374t^{2}-1215\tau^{2}\bigr)C^{3}

\displaystyle{}{+}\,\bigl(3840000\tau^{12}-423014400t^{2}\tau^{8}-10560000\tau^{10}-1700611200t^{4}\tau^{4}

\displaystyle{}\,\quad-235612800t^{2}\tau^{6}+7440000\tau^{8}-306110016t^{6}+2173003200t^{4}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle{}\,\quad-666646200t^{2}\tau^{4}+1440000\tau^{6}+25223400t^{2}\tau^{2}-2160000\tau^{4}\bigr)C^{2}

\displaystyle{}{+}\,\bigl(-10174464t\tau^{12}+403107840t^{3}\tau^{8}+60549120t\tau^{10}-3809369088t^{5}\tau^{4}

\displaystyle{}\,\quad+2205895680t^{3}\tau^{6}-197475840t\tau^{8}-3673320192t^{7}+8865853056t^{5}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle{}\,\quad-2979218880t^{3}\tau^{4}+147277440t\tau^{6}-25509168t^{5}+249930360t^{3}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle{}\,\quad-1451520t\tau^{4}+1275264t\tau^{2}\bigr)C

\displaystyle{}{-}\,65536\tau^{16}+5308416t^{2}\tau^{12}+1277952\tau^{14}-161243136t^{4}\tau^{8}-29859840t^{2}\tau^{10}

\displaystyle{}{-}\,7827456\tau^{12}+2176782336t^{6}\tau^{4}-362797056t^{4}\tau^{6}-21772800t^{2}\tau^{8}

\displaystyle{}{+}\,15861760\tau^{10}-11019960576t^{8}+8979227136t^{6}\tau^{2}-5048925696t^{4}\tau^{4}

\displaystyle{}{+}\,806993280t^{2}\tau^{6}-12437760\tau^{8}-153055008t^{6}+583036704t^{4}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle{}{+}\,42729120t^{2}\tau^{4}+2624256\tau^{6}-531441t^{4}+6601824t^{2}\tau^{2}

\displaystyle{}{+}\,546048\tau^{4}+20736\tau^{2}=0.

One can readily check that
\vec{r}_{{\rm low}}(b,c)
,
\vec{r}_{{\rm high}}(b,c)
(defined below), indeed both parametrize the above algebraic equation.

Appendix D
Parametrization of the phase space

Here we discuss the parametrization of
D_{q}
by (
3.4
)–(
3.6
) in more detail and prove Proposition
2.5
.

D.1
The critical surfaces

Let us start by studying the critical surfaces defined in Definition
2.4
.
Note that, by definition, the low and high temperature curves are given by the pair of parametric equations
\vec{r}_{\bullet}(b,c)=\langle\tau_{\bullet}(b,c),t_{\bullet}(b,c),q_{\bullet}(b,c)\rangle
,
\bullet\in\{{\rm low},{\rm high}\}
, defined by

\displaystyle\vec{r}_{{\rm low}}(b,c):=\Biggl\langle\frac{b^{3}}{\sqrt{N_{1}(b,c)N_{2}(b,c)}},-\frac{bc\bigl(b^{6}c^{2}+\frac{4}{3}b^{2}c^{2}+1\bigr)}{3N_{1}(b,c)N_{2}(b,c)},\frac{bcN_{2}(b,c)}{N_{1}(b,c)}\Biggr\rangle,

\displaystyle\vec{r}_{{\rm high}}(b,c):=\Biggl\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{M_{1}(b,c)M_{2}(b,c)}},-\frac{bc\bigl(4b^{2}c^{2}+3b^{2}+3c^{2}\bigr)}{9M_{1}(b,c)M_{2}(b,c)},\frac{cM_{2}(b,c)}{bM_{1}(b,c)}\Biggr\rangle,

respectively,
where

\displaystyle N_{1}(b,c)=2b^{4}c^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2},\qquad

\displaystyle N_{2}(b,c)=b^{4}+b^{2}c^{2}+2,

\displaystyle M_{1}(b,c)=b^{2}c^{2}+2c^{2}+1,\qquad

\displaystyle M_{2}(b,c)=b^{2}c^{2}+2b^{2}+1.

In both cases, the parameter range for
(b,c)
is
0<b<1
,
0<c<b
. The critical curve
\gamma_{b}
, defined as the boundary connecting the surfaces
S_{{\rm low}}
and
S_{{\rm high}}
, is given parametrically as

\vec{r}(c)=\Biggl\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{(3c^{2}+1)(c^{2}+3)}},-\frac{c\bigl(7c^{2}+3\bigr)}{9(c^{2}+3)(3c^{2}+1)},\frac{c\bigl(c^{2}+3\bigr)}{3c^{2}+1}\Biggr\rangle,\qquad 0<c<1,

which results from the limit
b\to 1
in either of the critical surfaces
\vec{r}_{{\rm low}}
,
\vec{r}_{{\rm high}}
.

Note that by changing variables from
(\tau,t,q)
to
\bigl(\tau^{2},t,q\bigr)
the parametrizations become rational and thus the surface
S_{{\rm low}}
and
S_{{\rm high}}
are algebraic.

Lemma D.1
.

The union
S_{{\rm low}}\cup S_{{\rm high}}
of the low- and high-temperature critical surface is the graph of a function of
0<\tau<1
and
0<q<1
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the projection of
S_{{\rm high}}\cup S_{{\rm low}}\cup\gamma_{b}
to the
t=0
plane is a bijection onto the square defined by
0<\tau<1
and
0<q<1
.

The projection of the surface
S_{{\rm high}}
to the
t=0
plane is obtained by setting
t=0
in its parameterization. The parameterization becomes singular near
b=c=0
, but this can be resolved by setting
\tilde{c}=c/b
, leading to the map

(\tau,q)=\Biggl(\frac{1}{\sqrt{(1+2\tilde{c}^{2}b^{2}+\tilde{c}^{2}b^{4})(1+2b^{2}+\tilde{c}^{2}b^{4})}},\frac{\tilde{c}\bigl(1+2b^{2}+\tilde{c}^{2}b^{4}\bigr)}{1+2\tilde{c}^{2}b^{2}+\tilde{c}^{2}b^{4}}\Biggr).

(D.1)

Note that this defines a smooth map on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, but we are mainly interested in its restriction to the closure of the parameter space given by unit square
0\leq b\leq 1
and
0\leq\tilde{c}<1
. The map
(b,\tilde{c})\mapsto(\tau,q)
is differentiable and the Jacobian never vanishes in the open square. This implies that it is locally a diffeomorphism. The image of the boundary is given by the following observations:

•

For
b=0
, we find
\tau=1
and
0\leq q\leq 1
.

•

For
b=1
, we obtain the projection of curve
\gamma_{b}
to the
t=0
plane.

•

For
\tilde{c}=0
, we find
q=0
and
1/\sqrt{3}\leq\tau\leq 1
.

•

For
\tilde{c}=1
, we find
q=1
and
1/4\leq\tau\leq 1
.

It is also easy to verify that the map (
D.1
) provides a bijection from the boundary of the unit square to the boundary of this region.

Concluding, (
D.1
) is a continuous map from the closed unit square, that is locally diffeomorphic and maps the boundary bijective to the boundary of the image. Since
\phi
is continuous and the unit square is compact, it is also a proper map (pre-image of compacts sets are compact). By furthering invoking the Hadamard–Caccioppoli theorem
[
60
]
, one find that the map defines a homeomorphism, from the closed unit disk onto its image. Moreover, the image of the boundary divides
\mathbb{R}^{2}
into two components and the image equals the bounded component. In other words, the projection of
S_{{\rm high}}
is the region enclosed by
\tau=1
,
q=0
,
q=1
and the projection of
\gamma_{b}
to the
t=0
plane.

In a similar fashion, one can show that the projection of
S_{{\rm low}}
is the region enclosed by
\tau=0
,
q=0
,
q=1
and the projection of
\gamma_{b}
to the
t=0
plane. The projections together thus fill out the unit square bijectively and this is proves the statement.
∎

D.2
Proof of Proposition
2.5

Proof.

We will characterize the image of the parametrization inspired by the following simple principle: if
D
is an open set with compact closure and
\phi\colon\overline{D}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a continuous function such that
\phi(D)
open, then
\partial\phi(D)\subset\phi(\partial D)
. Since the Jacobian (
2.4
) does not vanish in the interior of
R
, the map
\Pi
maps
R^{\circ}
to an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. However,
R
is not compact and
\Pi
is not continuous for
b\to 0
. Nevertheless, as we will show below by a limiting procedure, the boundary
\partial\Pi(R)
can still be found from the behavior of
\Pi
near the boundary
\partial R
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, define

R^{\varepsilon}=R\cap\{b>\varepsilon\}.

Then the closure of
R^{\varepsilon}
is compact and
\Pi
is continuous up to the boundary. The boundary of
\Pi(R^{\varepsilon})
consists of the images of the boundary part of
R^{\varepsilon}
given by respectively
a=b^{-1}
,
a=1
,
c=b
,
c=0
and
b=\varepsilon
. The first two conditions, lead to the critical surfaces
S_{{\rm low}}
and
S_{{\rm high}}
(note that for
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
this will only trace our part of these surface but when
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
we retrieve the entire surfaces again). The condition
c=b
gives us (part of) the plane
q=1
. The condition
c=0
turns out to be obsolete as it leads to both
{t=0}
and
q=0
and this is the part where the critical surfaces meet the
t=0
plane. So it remains to investigate the behavior of the image of
b=\varepsilon
when
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. Note that the image of
b=\varepsilon
under
\Pi
gives a smooth surface. We claim that for
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
this surface will converge to parts of the planes
\tau=0

and
t=0
.

To study the
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
limit we start, as in the proof of Lemma
D.1
, by setting

c=\tilde{c}b
,
and consider the parametrization by
a
,
b
and
\tilde{c}
. Now
0\leq\tilde{c}\leq 1
and the limit
b=\varepsilon\downarrow 0
simply gives

\tau(a,b,c)\to 1/a^{2},\qquad t(a,b,c)\to 0,\qquad q(a,b,c)\to\tilde{c}.

One can readily verify that this parameterizes the
t=0
wall of the phase space for
0<q<1
:

\{(\tau,t,q)\mid t=0,\,0<\tau<1,\,0<q<1\}.

The
\tau=0
plane can obtained in a similar way. To this end, set

a=\tilde{a}/b
,
\tilde{c}=c/b
,
with
0<\tilde{a}\leq 1
and
0\leq\tilde{c}\leq 1
.
Then for
b=\varepsilon\downarrow 0
we find

\tau(a,b,c)\to 0,\qquad t(a,b,c)\to-\frac{\tilde{a}^{2}\tilde{c}}{3(1+\tilde{a}^{2})(1+\tilde{a}^{2}\tilde{c}^{2})},\qquad q(a,b,c)\to\frac{\tilde{c}\bigl(1+\tilde{a}^{2}\bigr)}{1+\tilde{a}^{2}\tilde{c}^{2}}.

One can readily verify that the above parametrizes the following set:

\biggl\{(\tau,t,q)\mid\tau=0,\,-\frac{1}{12}q<t<0,\,0<q<1\biggr\}.

(D.2)

Equality
t=-q/12
in this limiting procedure is obtained by setting
\tilde{a}=1
and thus
a=b^{-1}
. Therefore, (
D.2
) is the part of the plane
\tau=0
enclosed by
t=0
,
q=0
,
q=1
and the part of the boundary of
S_{{\rm low}}
that is in the
\tau=0
plane.

Concluding, after taking the limit
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, we find that
\Pi
maps
R^{\circ}
to the open region in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for which the boundary consists of the critical surfaces
S_{{\rm low}}\cup S_{{\rm high}}\cup\gamma_{b}
and the four planes defined by respectively
q=0
,
q=1
,
t=0
and
\tau=0
.

Next we show that
\Pi(R)=D
. Note that, by Lemma
D.1
, we can write

\Pi(R)=\{(t,\tau,q)\mid 0<\tau<1,\,q\in\mathbb{R},\,t_{0}(\tau,q)<t<0\},

for some function
t_{0}
. It remains to show that
t_{0}(\tau,q)=t_{\rm cr}(\tau,q)
. We first note that it by a simple but tedious computation one can show that
\sigma=a^{2}bc
indeed solves (
2.1
), for
\tau
,
t
,
q
as defined in (
3.4
)–(
3.6
). It is also clear that
\sigma
depends continuously on
a
,
b
,
c
in the interior of
R
and for
{b\to 0}
we find
\sigma\to 0
. Moreover, since the Jacobian of
\Pi
is not vanishing in the interior of
R
, we see that
\sigma
is even differentiable as a function of
t
. We thus proved that
\sigma(t)=a^{2}bc
for
t_{0}\leq t<0
. Finally, at the boundary the Jacobian vanishes and
\sigma(t)=a^{2}bc
is no longer differentiable as a function of
t
and thus no longer analytic in
t
. By definition, we must have
{t_{\rm cr}=t_{0}}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Calculations from physics: the genus
\boldsymbol{0}
partition function

In this appendix, we study the genus
0
free energy, which is defined in terms of the partition function

\displaystyle Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;N):=\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}\!\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}\!\!\exp\biggl\{\tr\biggl[\tau XY-\frac{1}{2}X^{2}-\frac{1}{2}Y^{2}-\frac{t{\rm e}^{H}}{4N}X^{4}-\frac{t{\rm e}^{-H}}{4N}Y^{4}\biggr]\!\biggr\}{\rm d}X{\rm d}Y.\!\!

(E.1)

The (genus
0
) free energy is then defined as the ratio

F(\tau,t,H)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\log\frac{Z_{N}(\tau,t,H;N)}{Z_{N}(\tau,0,0;N)}.

More precisely, we reproduce the following results from the physics literature:

(1)

Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;N)
is a generating function for partition functions of the Ising model on genus
g

4
-regular graphs, in a sense we shall make precise. This is the result of Kazakov (for
H=0
) and Kazakov and Boulatov (
H\neq 0
)
[
14
,
57
]
. This is presented in Appendix
E.1
.

(2)

The large
N
limit of the genus
0
partition function admits an exact expression in terms of the solution to a certain implicit equation. This
is also the result of
[
14
,
57
]
. This is presented in Appendix
E.2
.

(3)

We check that the results of these calculations indeed agree. We calculate the first few terms of this generating function by hand, and show that this result agrees with the exact formula from
[
14
,
57
]
. This is presented in Appendix
E.3
.

(4)

The asymptotics of the Taylor coefficients of
\sigma(\tau,t,H)
are derived, and explicitly so for the special case
H=0
, which is again a result of
[
57
]
.

We present these results in more detail here, as the calculations are somewhat involved and the methods from the physics literature are perhaps unfamiliar to the target audience of this work. This appendix is meant to hopefully clarify these results; we do not claim any originality here. We also stress that these methods are formal: they are best thought of as guiding principles.

E.1
Wick expansion of the partition function

In this appendix, we replicate in detail the Wick expansion of the free energy, and show that it
is a formal generating function for the Ising model on random
4
-regular genus
g
graphs.
We begin by considering the Gaussian model:

{\rm d}\mathbb{P}(X,Y)=\frac{1}{Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;N)}\exp\biggl\{N\tr\biggl[\tau XY-\frac{1}{2}X^{2}-\frac{1}{2}Y^{2}\biggr]\biggr\}{\rm d}X{\rm d}Y.

(E.2)

Let
\langle\cdot\rangle_{n}
denote the expected value with respect to this measure. Then, one can directly compute the following.

Lemma E.1
.

The matrix elements
X_{ij}
,
Y_{ij}

have the following means and covariances:

\displaystyle\langle X_{ij}\rangle_{n}=0,

\displaystyle\langle Y_{ij}\rangle_{n}=0,

(E.3)

\displaystyle\langle X_{ij}X_{k\ell}\rangle_{n}=\langle Y_{ij}Y_{k\ell}\rangle_{n}=\frac{1}{N(1-\tau^{2})}\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jk},\qquad

\displaystyle\langle X_{ij}Y_{k\ell}\rangle_{n}=\langle Y_{ij}X_{k\ell}\rangle_{n}=\frac{\tau}{N(1-\tau^{2})}\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jk}.

Proof.

We only sketch the idea of the proof here. The main point is that one can explicitly compute the moment generating function of this model.
Let
J,K\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
be a fixed Hermitian matrices, and consider the quantity

f(J,K):=\langle\exp(\tr JX+KY)\rangle_{n},

where the expected value is taken with respect to the measure (
E.2
). By the usual trick of completing the square, one can compute that

f(J,K)=\exp\biggl\{\frac{1}{N(1-\tau^{2})}\tr\biggl[\frac{1}{2}J^{2}+\frac{1}{2}K^{2}-\tau JK\biggr]\biggr\}.

Taking derivatives with respect to the appropriate matrix elements of
J
,
K
yields (
E.3
).
∎

Now, since the entries of both
X
,
Y
are all centered Gaussian variables, we can apply Wick’s theorem to calculate the expected value of any higher order moment in terms of the covariances (
E.3
). Let us now explain how to calculate expected values of the form

\Biggl\langle\prod_{p=1}^{k}\tr X^{i_{p}}\prod_{q=1}^{\ell}\tr Y^{j_{q}}\Biggr\rangle_{n},

(E.4)

where
(i_{1},\dots,i_{p})
and
(j_{1},\dots,j_{p})
are sequences of positive integers. The main takeaway will be that one can interpret this expected value digramatically; we shall now explain this procedure. Let
K=\sum_{p=1}^{k}i_{p}
,
L=\sum_{q=1}^{\ell}j_{q}
, and
E=K+L
. We assume that
E
is even; otherwise, this expected value is automatically zero. We can apply Wick’s theorem to (
E.4
), and convert this expected value into a sum over all possible pairings of the indices:

\Biggl\langle\prod_{p=1}^{k}\tr X^{i_{p}}\prod_{q=1}^{\ell}\tr Y^{j_{q}}\Biggr\rangle_{n}=\sum_{I}\sum_{\pi\in\Pi_{E}}\prod_{\{(r_{a},r_{b}),(r_{c},r_{d})\}\in\pi}\langle Z_{r_{a}r_{b}}Z_{r_{c}r_{d}}\rangle_{n},

where
\Pi_{E}
denotes the set of pairings of
E
elements, the sum
I
runs over all of the indices of the traces
\tr X^{i_{p}}
,
p=1,\dots,k
, and
\tr Y^{j_{q}}
,
q=1,\dots,j
, and

\displaystyle Z_{r_{a}r_{b}}\!:=\!\begin{cases}X_{r_{a}r_{b}},&(r_{a},r_{b})\text{ belongs to the index set of one of the traces $\tr X^{i_{p}}$, $p\!=\!1,\dots,k$},\\
Y_{r_{a}r_{b}},&(r_{a},r_{b})\text{ belongs to the index set of one of the traces $\tr Y^{i_{q}}$, $q\!=\!1,\dots,j$}.\end{cases}

Interchanging the order of summation, we see that each pairing
\pi
contributing a factor of

w[R_{\pi}]:=\sum_{I}\prod_{\{(r_{a},r_{b}),(r_{c},r_{d})\}\in\pi}\langle Z_{r_{a}r_{b}}Z_{r_{c}r_{d}}\rangle_{n}

to the expected value. To each such pairing
\pi
, we associate a 2-colored ribbon graph
R_{\pi}
as follows:

(1)

Draw
k

X
-colored vertices, each with degree
i_{p}
,
p=1,\dots,k
, with half-edges labelled by the indices of the trace of the corresponding vertex.

(2)

Draw
\ell

Y
-colored vertices, each with degree
j_{q}
,
q=1,\dots,\ell
, with half-edges labelled by the indices of the trace of the corresponding vertex.

(3)

Pair the half-edges according to the pairing prescribed by
\pi
.

The resulting diagram is called a
2
-colored ribbon graph. We will sometimes denote a
2
-colored ribbon graph (associated to a pairing
\pi
)
on
V:=k+\ell
vertices, with
k
vertices of the first color, and
\ell:=V-k
of the second color, by
R_{\pi}(k,\ell)
. The
size
of such a ribbon graph is the number of vertices, denoted
|R_{\pi}(k,\ell)|=V
. We call such a coloring a
coloring of type
(k,\ell)
.
The contribution of such a graph to the Wick sum we will denote by
w[R_{\pi}(k,\ell)]
, or by
w[R_{\pi}]
, when the coloring type is
clear.

Let us compute more explicitly the contribution to the sum of a particular pairing (ribbon graph)
\pi
(
R_{\pi}
).
The ribbon graph associated to the pairing
\pi
will by construction have
V
vertices, and
E
edges, by the handshaking lemma.
Each face in the resulting ribbon graph corresponds to a collection of indices which will be identified by Wick’s theorem, since
\langle Z_{ab}Z_{cd}\rangle_{n}=\frac{\star}{n(1-\tau^{2})}\delta_{ad}\delta_{bc}
, where
Z
is either
X
,
Y
, and
\star
is either
1
or
\tau
. Thus,
the number of independent summation indices
I^{\prime}
left over after accounting for the identified indices is the same as the number of faces, which
we shall denote by
F
. The contribution will be multiplied by a factor of
\tau^{D}
, where
D
counts
the number of edges connecting
X
-colored vertices to
Y
-colored vertices. Thus, we see that
w[R_{\pi}]
is (upon identifying

N:=n
):

w[R_{\pi}]=\frac{1}{n^{E}}\biggl(\frac{1}{1-\tau^{2}}\biggr)^{E}n^{F}\tau^{D}=\biggl(\frac{1}{1-\tau^{2}}\biggr)^{E}n^{\chi(R_{\pi})-V}\tau^{D}.

Let
U
denote the number of edges in
R_{\pi}
connecting
X
-colored vertices to
X
-colored vertices and
Y
-colored vertices to

Y
-colored vertices.
Then, we have the equality
D+U=E
, the total number of edges in
R_{\pi}
. If we define
S:=D-U
, then
D=\frac{1}{2}(E+S)
, and
we can express the weight
w[R_{\pi}]
finally as

w[R_{\pi}]=\biggl(\frac{\tau^{1/2}}{1-\tau^{2}}\biggr)^{E}n^{\chi(R_{\pi})-V}\tau^{\frac{1}{2}S}.

(E.5)

Note that, since
E
is always even, as the graph comes from a pairing, so there is no problem defining the square root.
What we have proven is the following proposition:

Proposition E.2
.

Let
X
,
Y
be Gaussian random matrices, distributed according to the measure (
E.2
), with parameter
N:=n
. Let

(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
,
(j_{1},\dots,j_{\ell})
be a
k
-
(
respectively,
\ell)
-tuple of positive integers, and set
V:=k+\ell
. Furthermore, put

E:=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sum_{p}i_{p}+\sum_{q}j_{q}\bigr)
. Then,

n^{V}\Biggl\langle\prod_{p=1}^{k}\tr X^{i_{p}}\prod_{q=1}^{\ell}\tr Y^{j_{q}}\Biggr\rangle_{n}=\biggl(\frac{\tau^{1/2}}{1-\tau^{2}}\biggr)^{E}\sum_{g\geq 0}\frac{e_{g}(\tau)}{n^{2g-2}},

where
e_{g}(\tau)
is a polynomial in
\tau
, defined by

e_{g}(\tau)=\sum_{R_{\pi}}\tau^{\frac{1}{2}S(R_{\pi})},

where the sum runs over all genus
g
unordered
(k,\ell)
-colored ribbon graphs, and
S(R_{\pi})
is the number of edges in
R_{\pi}
between
like-colored vertices minus the number of edges between unalike vertices.

Now, let us apply this proposition to the matrix integral (
E.1
).
Expanding
\exp\bigl[-\frac{t}{4N}\tr X^{4}\bigr]
,
\exp\bigl[-\frac{t}{4N}\tr Y^{4}\bigr]
as series in
t
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\exp\biggl[-\frac{t}{4N}\tr X^{4}\biggr]\exp\biggl[-\frac{t}{4N}\tr Y^{4}\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-t/4N)^{V}}{V!}\sum_{k+j=V}\frac{V!}{k!j!}\bigl(\tr X^{4}\bigr)^{k}\bigl(\tr Y^{4}\bigr)^{j}{\rm e}^{(k-j)H}.

If we insert this expression into equation (
E.1
), and divide by the Gaussian partition function

Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;N)
, we obtain that

\frac{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;N)}{Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;N)}=\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-t/4N)^{V}}{V!}\sum_{k+j=V}\frac{V!}{k!j!}\bigl\langle\bigl(\tr X^{4}\bigr)^{k}\bigl(\tr Y^{4}\bigr)^{j}\bigr\rangle_{n}{\rm e}^{(k-j)H},

where the expected value here is taken with respect to the Gaussian measure (
E.2
).
It is important to notice that, provided
k+j=V
,
\frac{V!}{k!j!}
counts the number of 2-colorings of
V
objects, with
k
of the first color
and
j
of the second. Expanding the expected value
\bigl\langle\bigl(\tr X^{4}\bigr)^{k}\bigl(\tr Y^{4}\bigr)^{j}\bigr\rangle_{n}
using Wick’s theorem and the diagrammatic rules
we established, we obtain that

\frac{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;N)}{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;N)}=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-t/4N)^{V}}{V!}\sum_{k+j=V}\frac{V!}{k!j!}\sum_{|R_{\pi}(k,j)|=V}w[R_{\pi}(k,j)]{\rm e}^{(k-j)H},

where the innermost sum runs over all
2
-colored ribbon graphs on
V
vertices with
k
edges of the first color and
j
of the second,
k+j=V
.
The colorings in this case are
unlabelled
, in the sense that any graph of a fixed type with coloring
(k,j)
are considered to be the same.
However, we can count
labelled
colorings by noticing that

(1)

If we color any labelled ribbon graph
R_{\pi}
in two different ways with precisely
k
vertices of the first color and
j
of the second, the resulting weights these diagrams contribute to the sum are identical.

(2)

If
k+j=V
, there are precisely
\frac{V!}{k!j!}
possible colorings of type
(k,j)
on a given graph on
V
vertices.

Thus, we see that inner sum over
k
,
j
can be interpreted as a sum over the distinct possible colorings of the vertices, and we have that

\frac{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;N)}{Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;N)}=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-t/4N)^{V}}{V!}\sum_{|R_{\pi}|=V}w_{h}[R_{\pi}],

where we have defined a modified weight function

w_{h}[R_{\pi}]:=w[R_{\pi}]{\rm e}^{(k-j)H},

and the internal sum now runs over all
labelled,
2
-colored
ribbon graphs on
V
-vertices, with
any
coloring scheme.
For our purposes, it is useful switch the order of summation over colorings and graphs, i.e., to write the above as

\frac{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;N)}{Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;N)}=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-t/4N)^{V}}{V!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ribbon}\\
\text{graphs}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{colorings of}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}w_{h}[R_{\pi}]{\rm e}^{(k-j)H},

where the sum

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ribbon}\\
\text{graphs}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}

is a sum over all
uncolored, labelled
ribbon graphs on
V
vertices, and

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{colorings of}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}

runs over all possible
2
-colorings of
R_{\pi}
. Now, using the formula for the weights
w[R_{\pi}]
of colored ribbon graphs
we derived earlier (cf. equation (
E.5
)), and using the fact that
E=2V
by the handshaking lemma, we have that
(again putting
N:=n
):

\frac{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;n)}{Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;n)}=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{V!}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{V}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ribbon}\\
\text{graphs}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}n^{\chi(R_{\pi})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{colorings of}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}\tau^{\frac{1}{2}S(R_{\pi})}{\rm e}^{(k-j)H}.

For a fixed graph
R_{\pi}
, this sum is nothing but the partition function for the Ising model on this graph, with the parameter identification

\tau={\rm e}^{-2\beta}
,
H=\beta h
. Thus, we have shown that

\frac{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;n)}{Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;n)}=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{V!}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{V}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ribbon}\\
\text{graphs}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}n^{\chi(R_{\pi})}Z_{R_{\pi}}(\beta,h).

A general principle in the theory of generating functions is the following. Suppose we have an exponential generating function which counts the number of labelled objects. Then, its logarithm counts the number of
connected
objects of the same kind. For more details pertaining to this fact, one may consult, for example, the book
[
78
, Section 5]
, instead a sum over
connected
ribbon graphs:

\log\frac{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;n)}{Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;n)}=\sum_{V=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{V!}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{V}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ribbon}\\
\text{graphs}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{}^{{}^{\prime}}n^{\chi(R_{\pi})}Z_{R_{\pi}}(\beta,h),

where
′
denotes the sum only over connected diagrams, and
\beta
,
h
are related to
\tau
,
H
via the formulae

\tau={\rm e}^{-2\beta},\qquad H=\beta h.

In particular, if we divide through by
n^{2}
, and take a limit as
n\to\infty
, we obtain that

\displaystyle F(\tau,t,H):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\log\frac{Z_{n}(\tau,t,H;n)}{Z_{n}(\tau,0,0;n)}=\sum_{V=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{V!}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{V}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{ribbon}\\
\text{graphs}\\
R_{\pi}\end{subarray}}{}^{{}^{\prime}}Z_{R_{\pi}}(\beta,h),

(E.6)

where the internal sum runs over all connected,
4
-valent planar ribbon graphs on
V
vertices.

E.2
Formal calculation of the genus
\boldsymbol{0}
partition function

Here we calculate the genus
0
partition function in the limit as
N:=n
tends to infinity.
Define the function
V(z;t):=\frac{1}{2}z^{2}+\frac{t}{4}z^{4}
, and put

W(x,y):=\tau xy-V\bigl(x,t{\rm e}^{H}\bigr)-V\bigl(y,t{\rm e}^{-H}\bigr).

We consider the family of biorthogonal polynomials defined by the formula

h_{k}(\tau,t,H;N)\delta_{kj}=h_{k}\delta_{kj}=\iint P_{k}(x)Q_{k}(y){\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y,

where the integration is carried out over an appropriately chosen contour, so that the above integral makes sense. The genus zero generating function for
the Ising model on random spherical quadrangulations is then

F(\tau,t,H):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\log\prod_{k=0}^{N}\frac{h_{k}(\tau,t,H;N)}{h_{k}(\tau,0,0;N)}.

In order to calculate
F(\tau,t,H)
, we must first study some properties of the corresponding biorthogonal polynomials. We have the following lemma.

Lemma E.3
.

The polynomials
\{P_{k}(x)\}
,
\{Q_{k}(y)\}
satisfy the recursion relations

\displaystyle xP_{k}(x)=P_{k+1}(x)+R_{k}P_{k-1}(x)+S_{k}P_{k-3}(x),

(E.7)

\displaystyle yQ_{k}(y)=Q_{k+1}(y)+\tilde{R}_{k}Q_{k-1}(y)+\tilde{S}_{k}Q_{k-3}(y).

(E.8)

Proof.

We sketch the proof of (
E.7
). Obviously,
xP_{k}(x)=P_{k+1}(x)+\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{jk}P_{j}(x)
. Multiplying this relation by
Q_{\ell}(y)
and integrating against the measure
{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y
, one obtains

c_{k\ell}h_{\ell}=\iint xP_{k}(x)Q_{\ell}(y){\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

Using the identity
x{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}=\frac{1}{N\tau}\bigl(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}\bigr)+\frac{1}{\tau}\bigl(y+t{\rm e}^{-H}y^{3}\bigr)
, one sees
that

c_{k\ell}h_{\ell}=\iint P_{k}(x)Q_{\ell}(y)\biggl[\frac{1}{N\tau}\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}\biggr)+\frac{1}{\tau}\bigl(y+t{\rm e}^{-H}y^{3}\bigr)\biggr]{\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

Since
\ell<k+1
, the first term vanishes identically, since integration by parts yields the integrand as
-P_{k}(x)Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(y){\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}
,
and
Q_{\ell}^{\prime}(y)
is a polynomial of degree less than
k
. Similarly, last two terms can only be nonzero if
\ell=k,k-1,k-3
; the
k
term is also
zero, by the symmetry of the integration measure upon the change of variables
(x,y)\to(-x,-y)
. The result (
E.8
) is obtained in an identical manner.
∎

We now show that the recursion coefficients satisfy additional relations.

Lemma E.4
.

Define
f_{k}:=h_{k}/h_{k-1}
. The coefficients
R_{k}
,
S_{k}
,
\tilde{R}_{k}
,
\tilde{S}_{k}
satisfy the following relations:

\displaystyle\tau S_{k}=t{\rm e}^{-H}f_{k}f_{k-1}f_{k-2},

(E.9)

\displaystyle\tau R_{k}=f_{k}\bigl[1+t{\rm e}^{-H}\bigl(\tilde{R}_{k+1}+\tilde{R}_{k}+\tilde{R}_{k-1}\bigr)\bigr],

(E.10)

\displaystyle\frac{k}{N}=-\tau f_{k}+\tilde{R}_{k}+t{\rm e}^{-H}\bigl[\tilde{S}_{k+2}+\tilde{S}_{k+1}+\tilde{S}_{k}+\tilde{R}_{k}\bigl(\tilde{R}_{k+1}+\tilde{R}_{k}+\tilde{R}_{k-1}\bigr)\bigr],

(E.11)

\displaystyle\tau\tilde{S}_{k}=t{\rm e}^{H}f_{k}f_{k-1}f_{k-2},

(E.12)

\displaystyle\tau\tilde{R}_{k}=f_{k}\bigl[1+t{\rm e}^{H}\bigl({R}_{k+1}+{R}_{k}+{R}_{k-1}\bigr)\bigr],

(E.13)

\displaystyle\frac{k}{N}=-\tau f_{k}+{R}_{k}+t{\rm e}^{H}\bigl[{S}_{k+2}+{S}_{k+1}+{S}_{k}+{R}_{k}\bigl({R}_{k+1}+{R}_{k}+{R}_{k-1}\bigr)\bigr].

(E.14)

Proof.

We only prove the first relation; the last three are proven identically. Multiplying equation (
E.7
) by
\tau Q_{k-3}(y)
, and integrating with respect to the measure
{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y
, we find that

\tau S_{k}h_{k-3}=\iint\tau xP_{k}(x)Q_{k-3}(y){\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

Using the identity
\tau x{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}=\frac{1}{N}\bigl(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}\bigr)+\bigl(y+t{\rm e}^{-H}y^{3}\bigr)
, we see that

\tau S_{k}h_{k-3}=\iint\tau P_{k}(x)Q_{k-3}(y)\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial y}{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}\biggr)+\bigl(y+t{\rm e}^{-H}y^{3}\bigr)\biggr]{\rm d}x{\rm d}y.

The first term drops out upon integrating by parts; similarly, since
\deg[yQ_{k-3}]=k-2
, the second term also evaluates to
0
. On the
last term, we use iteratively the recursion formula (
E.8
):

\displaystyle\tau S_{k}h_{k-3}

\displaystyle{}=t{\rm e}^{-H}\iint P_{k}(x)\left[Q_{k}(y)+\text{lower degree polynomials }\right]{\rm e}^{NW(x,y)}{\rm d}x{\rm d}y

\displaystyle{}=t{\rm e}^{-H}h_{k}.

∎

In planar limit, we assume the scaling
\frac{k}{N}\to\lambda
,
f_{k}\to f(\lambda)
, and so on. The above formulas (
E.9
)–(
E.14
) imply that, in the limit
as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\tau S(\lambda)=t{\rm e}^{-H}f(\lambda)^{3},

\displaystyle\tau R(\lambda)=f(\lambda)\bigl[1+3t{\rm e}^{-H}\tilde{R}(\lambda)\bigr],

\displaystyle\lambda=-\tau f(\lambda)+\tilde{R}(\lambda)+3t{\rm e}^{-H}\bigl[\tilde{S}(\lambda)+\tilde{R}(\lambda)^{2}\bigr],

(E.15)

\displaystyle\tau\tilde{S}(\lambda)=t{\rm e}^{H}f(\lambda)^{3},

\displaystyle\tau\tilde{R}(\lambda)=f(\lambda)\bigl[1+3t{\rm e}^{H}{R}(\lambda)\bigr],

\displaystyle\lambda=-\tau f(\lambda)+{R}(\lambda)+3t{\rm e}^{H}\bigl[S(\lambda)+R(\lambda)^{2}\bigr],

(E.16)

and these relations we can solve for
S(\lambda)
,
R(\lambda)
,
\tilde{R}(\lambda)
, and
\tilde{S}(\lambda)
in terms of
f(\lambda)
,
and the parameters
\tau
,
t
,
H
:

\displaystyle S(\lambda)=\frac{t{\rm e}^{-H}}{\tau}f(\lambda)^{3},\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{S}(\lambda)=\frac{t{\rm e}^{H}}{\tau}f(\lambda)^{3},

\displaystyle R(\lambda)=\frac{\tau+3t{\rm e}^{-H}f(\lambda)}{\tau^{2}-9t^{2}f(\lambda)^{2}}f(\lambda),\qquad

\displaystyle\tilde{R}(\lambda)=\frac{\tau+3t{\rm e}^{H}f(\lambda)}{\tau^{2}-9t^{2}f(\lambda)^{2}}f(\lambda).

These formulae can then be subsequently substituted into either equations (
E.15
) or (
E.16
) (they yield the same equation in the end) to obtain an
implicit formula for the function
f(\lambda)=f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)
:

\displaystyle\lambda=-\tau f(\lambda)+\frac{\tau+3t{\rm e}^{-H}f(\lambda)}{\tau^{2}-9t^{2}f(\lambda)^{2}}f(\lambda)+\frac{3t{\rm e}^{H}\bigl(\tau+3t{\rm e}^{-H}f(\lambda)\bigr)^{2}}{(\tau^{2}-9t^{2}f(\lambda)^{2})^{2}}f(\lambda)^{2}+\frac{3t^{2}f(\lambda)^{3}}{\tau}

(E.17)

\displaystyle\qquad{}\Longleftrightarrow\lambda=-\tau f(\lambda)+\frac{3t^{2}}{\tau}f(\lambda)^{3}+\frac{\tau f(\lambda)}{(\tau-3tf(\lambda))^{2}}+\frac{6\tau^{2}tf(\lambda)^{2}}{(\tau^{2}-9t^{2}f(\lambda)^{2})^{2}}[\cosh H-1].

In particular, when
t=H=0
, we obtain the solution

f(\lambda;\tau,0,0)=\frac{\tau\lambda}{1-\tau^{2}}.

Returning again to the calculation of the function
F(\tau,t,H)
, note that, by the definition of the variables
f_{k}
,
\prod_{k=0}^{N}h_{k}=h_{0}^{N}\prod_{k=1}^{N}f_{k}^{N-k}
, and so

\frac{1}{N^{2}}\log\prod_{k=0}^{N}\frac{h_{k}(\tau,t,H;N)}{h_{k}(\tau,0,0;N)}=\frac{1}{N}\log\frac{h_{0}(\tau,t,H;N)}{h_{0}(\tau,0,0;N)}+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\biggl(1-\frac{k}{N}\biggr)\log\frac{f_{k}(\tau,t,H;N)}{f_{k}(\tau,0,0;N)}\frac{1}{N}.

Taking the limit as
N\to\infty
, the first term vanishes (the argument of the logarithm is bounded), and the second term approximates a
Riemann integral; we thus obtain the formula

\displaystyle F(\tau,t,H)

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\log\prod_{k=0}^{N}\frac{h_{k}(\tau,t,H;N)}{h_{k}(\tau,0,0;N)}=\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log\frac{f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)}{f(\lambda;\tau,0,0)}{\rm d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\tau}{1-\tau^{2}}+\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log f(\lambda;\tau,t,H){\rm d}\lambda.

(E.18)

This, along with the implicit formula (
E.17
), are enough (in principle) to determine the function
F(\tau,t,H)
. Integrating the above formula by parts,

\displaystyle F(\tau,t,H)={}

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\tau}{1-\tau^{2}}+\biggl[\biggl(\lambda-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\biggr)\log f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)\biggr]_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle{}{-}\,\int_{0}^{1}\biggl(\lambda-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\biggr)\frac{f^{\prime}(\lambda;\tau,t,H)}{f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)}{\rm d}\lambda

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\tau}{1-\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\log f(1;\tau,t,H)-\int_{0}^{1}\biggl(\lambda-\frac{1}{2}\lambda^{2}\biggr)\frac{f^{\prime}(\lambda;\tau,t,H)}{f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)}{\rm d}\lambda

(Here we have used the fact that
f(0;\tau,t,H)\equiv 0
). Making the change of variables
u=f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)
,

\frac{{\rm d}u}{u}=\frac{f^{\prime}}{f}{\rm d}\lambda
,

\displaystyle F(\tau,t,H)=\frac{3}{4}-\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\tau}{1-\tau^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\log f(1;\tau,t,H)-\int_{0}^{f(1;\tau,t,H)}\biggl(\tilde{\lambda}(u)-\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\lambda}(u)^{2}\biggr)\frac{{\rm d}u}{u},

where
\tilde{\lambda}(u)
is defined by use of the implicit equation (
E.17
):

\tilde{\lambda}(u)=-\tau u+\frac{3t^{2}}{\tau}u^{3}+\frac{\tau u}{(\tau-3tu)^{2}}+\frac{6\tau^{2}tu^{2}}{(\tau^{2}-9t^{2}u^{2})^{2}}[\cosh H-1].

The integration can be carried out directly, since
\lambda(u)
is a rational function. Finally, defining

\sigma(\tau,t,H):=-\frac{3t}{\tau}f(1;\tau,t,H),

and making an appropriate change of variables, we obtain the following Proposition:

Proposition E.5
.

The genus
0
partition function admits the expression

F(\tau,t,H)=\frac{3}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\log\frac{\bigl(1-\tau^{2}\bigr)\sigma(\tau,t,H)}{-3t}-\int_{0}^{\sigma(\tau,t,H)}\biggl(\lambda(u)-\frac{1}{2}\lambda(u)^{2}\biggr)\frac{{\rm d}u}{u},

where
\lambda(u)
is the rational function

\lambda(u)=-\frac{1}{t}\biggl[\frac{1}{9}\tau^{2}u\bigl(u^{2}-3\bigr)+\frac{1}{3}\frac{u}{(u+1)^{2}}-\frac{2}{3}\biggl(\frac{u}{u^{2}-1}\biggr)^{2}[\cosh H-1]\biggr]

and
\sigma=\sigma(\tau,t,H)
is defined implicitly by the equation

t=-\frac{1}{9}\tau^{2}\sigma\bigl(\sigma^{2}-3\bigr)-\frac{1}{3}\frac{\sigma}{(1+\sigma)^{2}}+\frac{2}{3}\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{1-\sigma^{2}}\biggr)^{2}[\cosh H-1].

This is the result originally obtained by Kazakov (
H=0
, cf.
[
57
]
) and Boulatov (
H\neq 0
, cf.
[
14
]
). If one expands
the function
F(\tau,t,H)
as a series in
t
, one obtains that

\displaystyle F(\tau,t,H)={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}4\tau^{-1}\cosh{H}}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bigl(8\tau^{2}+64+72\tau^{-2}\cosh(2H)\bigr)}\frac{1}{2}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad{}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\biggl(\frac{3456}{\tau^{3}}\cosh(H)\bigl(2\cosh(2H)+\tau^{4}+2\tau^{2}-1\bigr)\biggr)}\frac{1}{6}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{3}+\OO\bigl(t^{4}\bigr).

(E.19)

E.3
Comparison with explicit calculation

On the other hand, we can compute the first few contributions to the genus
0
free energy by hand.
The following graphs (shown in Figure
16
) contribute at genus zero:

Figure 16:
The isomorphism classes of graphs contributing at genus
0
to the free energy, up to
V=3
vertices. Each isomorphism class of graph contributes a factor of
N_{i}\cdot Z_{V}^{(i)}(\tau,H)
.

V=1
.

For one vertex, there is one type of graph contributing, shown in the figure. The partition function for the Ising model on this graph is, in the variables
\tau
,
H
,

Z_{1}^{(1)}(\tau,H)=2\tau^{-1}\cosh H.

V=2
.

For two vertices, there are two nonisomorphic types of graphs contributing, shown in the figure. Their corresponding partition functions are

Z_{2}^{(1)}(\tau,H)=2\tau^{-2}\cosh(2H)+2\tau^{2},\qquad Z_{2}^{(2)}(\tau,H)=2\tau^{-2}\cosh(2H)+2.

V=3
.

There are four nonisomorphic types of graphs contributing, shown in the figure. Their partition functions are

\displaystyle Z_{3}^{(1)}(\tau,H)=2\tau^{-3}\cosh(3H)+6\tau\cosh(H),

\displaystyle Z_{3}^{(2)}(\tau,H)=2\tau^{-3}\cosh(3H)+2\tau^{-1}\cosh(H)+4\tau\cosh(H),

\displaystyle Z_{3}^{(3)}(\tau,H)=2\tau^{-3}\cosh(3H)+6\tau^{-1}\cosh(H),

\displaystyle Z_{3}^{(4)}(\tau,H)=2\tau^{-3}\cosh(3H)+4\tau^{-1}\cosh(H)+2\tau\cosh(H).

Observe that we can write the genus zero free energy (
E.6
) equivalently as

F(\tau,t,H)=\sum_{V=0}^{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\biggl(\sum_{|G|=V}N_{G}Z_{G}(\tau,H)\biggr)}\frac{1}{V!}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{V},

where the internal sum is taken over
isomorphism classes
of graphs
G
on
V
vertices, and
N_{G}
counts the number of labelled graphs isomorphic to
G
when one “forgets”
the labelling. In this way, one can explicitly compute that

\displaystyle F(\tau,t,H)={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bigl[2\cdot Z_{1}^{(1)}(\tau,H)\bigr]}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{F(\tau,t,H) =}{}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bigl[4\cdot Z_{2}^{(1)}(\tau,H)+32\cdot Z_{2}^{(2)}(\tau,H)\bigr]}\frac{1}{2}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\hphantom{F(\tau,t,H) =}{}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bigl[64\cdot Z_{3}^{(1)}(\tau,H)+512\cdot Z_{3}^{(2)}(\tau,H)+384\cdot Z_{3}^{(3)}(\tau,H)+768\cdot Z_{3}^{(4)}(\tau,H)\bigr]}

\displaystyle\hphantom{F(\tau,t,H) =}{}\quad\times\frac{1}{6}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{3}+\OO\bigl(t^{4}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hphantom{F(\tau,t,H)}{}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}4\tau^{-1}\cosh{H}}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\bigl(8\tau^{2}+64+72\tau^{-2}\cosh(2H)\bigr)}\frac{1}{2}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{2}

\displaystyle\hphantom{F(\tau,t,H) =}{}\qquad{}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\biggl(\frac{3456}{\tau^{3}}\cosh(H)\bigl(2\cosh(2H)+\tau^{4}+2\tau^{2}-1\bigr)\!\biggr)}\frac{1}{6}\biggl(\frac{-t\tau}{4(1-\tau^{2})^{2}}\biggr)^{3}\!\!+\OO\bigl(t^{4}\bigr),

which is in agreement with the formula (
E.19
) originally obtained by Kazakov.

Remark E.6
.

High/low temperature limits of the free energy.

As a final remark, let us show that in the limit
\tau,H\to 0
, the free energy of the quartic-two matrix model reduces to twice the free energy of the
quartic
1
-matrix model. Intuitively, one should expect this result because
\tau
acts as a coupling parameter between the matrices
X
and
Y
; when

\tau,H\to 0
, we obtain two independent copies of the quartic
1
-matrix model. Recall that the free energy for the quartic
1
-matrix model is

\mathcal{F}(t):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\log\frac{Z^{(1)}_{N}(t;N)}{Z^{(1)}_{N}(0;N)},

where
Z^{(1)}_{n}(t;N)
is given by the formula

Z^{(1)}_{n}(t;N)=\int_{\mathcal{H}_{n}}\exp\biggl[-N\tr\biggl(\frac{1}{2}X^{2}+\frac{t}{4}X^{4}\biggr)\biggr]{\rm d}X.

Explicitly, one may evaluate
\mathcal{F}(t)
to be (cf., for example,
[
11
]
, or the more recent
[
13
]
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(t)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log\frac{-1+\sqrt{1+12t\lambda}}{6t\lambda}{\rm d}\lambda.

On the other hand, let us return to the formula for the partition function of the
2
-matrix model (see the first expression in equation (
E.18
)):

\displaystyle F(\tau,t,H)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log\frac{f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)}{f(\lambda;\tau,0,0)}{\rm d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log\frac{\bigl(1-\tau^{2}\bigr)f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)}{\tau\lambda}{\rm d}\lambda,

where
f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)
is defined by the implicit equation

\lambda=-\tau f(\lambda)+\frac{\tau+3t{\rm e}^{-H}f(\lambda)}{\tau^{2}-9t^{2}f(\lambda)^{2}}f(\lambda)+\frac{3t{\rm e}^{H}\bigl(\tau+3t{\rm e}^{-H}f(\lambda)\bigr)^{2}}{(\tau^{2}-9t^{2}f(\lambda)^{2})^{2}}f(\lambda)^{2}+\frac{3t^{2}f(\lambda)^{3}}{\tau}.

(E.20)

Define the new variable
\tilde{f}(\lambda;\tau,t,H):=\frac{(1-\tau^{2})}{\tau}f(\lambda;\tau,t,H)
. The reason for this change of variables is that
\tilde{f}(\lambda;\tau,t,H)\to\lambda
when
t\to 0
, i.e., the limit function is independent of
\tau
. Under this change of variables, equation (
E.20
) becomes

0=\lambda-\frac{\bigl(1+3t{\rm e}^{-H}\tilde{f}\bigr)\tilde{f}}{1-9t^{2}\tilde{f}^{2}}-\frac{3t{\rm e}^{H}\bigl(1+3t{\rm e}^{-H}\tilde{f}\bigr)^{2}}{\bigl(1-9t^{2}\tilde{f}^{2}\bigr)^{2}}+\OO\bigl(\tau^{2}\bigr).

In the limit as
\tau,H\to 0
, one sees that

0=\lambda-\frac{\bigl(1+3t\tilde{f}\bigr)\tilde{f}}{1-9t^{2}\tilde{f}^{2}}-\frac{3t\bigl(1+3t\tilde{f}\bigr)^{2}}{\bigl(1-9t^{2}\tilde{f}^{2}\bigr)^{2}}.

This equation can be solved for
\tilde{f}
:

\tilde{f}(t)=\frac{1+6t\lambda-\sqrt{1+12t\lambda}}{18t^{2}\lambda},

where we have taken the branch of the solution which tends to
\lambda
as
t\to 0
.
Inserting this expression into the expression for the free energy of the
2
-matrix model,

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau,H\to 0}F(\tau,t,H)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\tau,H\to 0}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log\frac{\tilde{f}(\tau,t,H)}{\lambda}{\rm d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log\frac{1+6t\lambda-\sqrt{1+12t\lambda}}{18t^{2}\lambda^{2}}{\rm d}\lambda

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log\biggl(\frac{-1+\sqrt{1+12t\lambda}}{6t\lambda}\biggr)^{2}{\rm d}\lambda

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{1}(1-\lambda)\log\frac{-1+\sqrt{1+12t\lambda}}{6t\lambda}{\rm d}\lambda

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{F}(t).

More generally, we have the following limits:

\displaystyle\lim_{H\to 0}\lim_{\tau\to 0}F\bigl(\tau,\bigl(1-\tau^{2}\bigr)t,H\bigr)=\lim_{H\to 0}\bigl[\mathcal{F}\bigl({\rm e}^{H}t\bigr)+\mathcal{F}\bigl({\rm e}^{-H}t\bigr)\bigr]=2\mathcal{F}(t),

\displaystyle\lim_{H\to 0}\lim_{\tau\to 1}F\bigl(\tau,\bigl(1-\tau^{2}\bigr)t,H\bigr)=\lim_{H\to 0}\bigl[\mathcal{F}\bigl(2{\rm e}^{H}t\bigr)+\mathcal{F}\bigl(2{\rm e}^{-H}t\bigr)\bigr]=2\mathcal{F}(2t).

The first limit follows from our previous considerations, without setting
H=0
. The replacement of
t
by
\bigl(1-\tau^{2}\bigr)t
here is natural from the point
of view of the graphical expansion of the
2
-matrix model. The second limit follows from identical calculations. These limits correspond to the low and high temperature limits of the Ising model. As originally pointed out by Kazakov
[
57
]
, the factor of
2
appearing in the
\tau\to 1
limit comes from the fact that, for a graph
G
on
V
vertices, the partition function for the Ising model on that graph
(
recalling that
\tau={\rm e}^{-2\beta}
)

\lim_{\beta\to 0}Z_{G}(\beta,h)\to 2^{V}.

Thus, in the limit as
\tau\to 1
, each planar,
4
-regular graph on
V
vertices should contributes the same factor of
2^{V}
to the free energy.

E.4
Large
\boldsymbol{V}
asymptotics of
\boldsymbol{\sigma(\tau,t,H)}

Here we calculate the large
V
asymptotics of the implicitly defined function
\sigma(\tau,t,H)
. The reason for these calculations is the following: in the works
[
14
,
57
]
, it is argued that the asymptotics of the Taylor coefficients of
F(\tau,t,H)
are dominated by the nearest critical point to the origin of the equation
\mathfrak{I}(\sigma;\tau,t,H)=0
. The authors make the assertion that the Taylor coefficients of
F
behave like
F_{V}\sim{\rm const}\cdot V^{\alpha}[g(\tau,H)]^{V}
; it is eventually the expression
g(\tau,H)
that they are interested in. Indeed, calculation of
g(\tau,H)
is equivalent to finding the radius of convergence of
F
, considered
as an analytic function of
t
. Since
F
is defined in terms of
\sigma(\tau,t,H)
, it is therefore plausible that this radius of convergence should coincide with the radius of convergence of
\sigma(\tau,t,H)
as a function of
t
. However, we were unfortunately unable to show
that this was the case, as it is not obvious that terms such as
\frac{1}{(\sigma+1)^{3}}
,
\log\frac{(1-\tau^{2})\sigma}{-3t}
do
not alter the radius of convergence of
F
(the remaining terms in the expression for
F
are polynomial in
\sigma
, and thus have the same radius of convergence as that of
\sigma
). We therefore instead have opted to simply investigate the Taylor coefficients of
\sigma(\tau,t,H)
as a function of
t
, and take the assumptions Kazakov makes about the implication of this function as fact.

Let us now set about calculating the Taylor coefficients of
\sigma(\tau,t,H)
as a function of
t
. We do so explicitly in the case when

H=0
, and also provide an expansion which holds as
H\to 0
. Recall that
\sigma=\sigma(\tau,t,H)
is defined by the equation

\mathfrak{I}(\sigma;\tau,t,H)=-\frac{1}{9}\tau^{2}\sigma\bigl(\sigma^{2}-3\bigr)-\frac{1}{3}\frac{\sigma}{(1+\sigma)^{2}}+\frac{2}{3}\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{1-\sigma^{2}}\biggr)^{2}[\cosh H-1]-t=0.

When
H=0
, the last term vanishes identically. Note that, for any fixed
\tau
,
H
, the above implicit equation is of special form: it
is actually an equation defining an
inverse
function to
t(\sigma)
.
We recall the following theorem, due to Lagrange (cf., for instance,
[
82
, p. 149, Exercise 25]
):

Theorem E.7

(Lagrange inversion formula)
.

Suppose
t=G(\sigma)
is analytic in a neighborhood of
\sigma=0
, and suppose
G(0)=0
,
G^{\prime}(0)\neq 0
. Then, an inverse function
\sigma=\sigma(t)
exists in a neighborhood of
t=0
, and is given by the power series

\sigma(t)=\sum_{V=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{V}\frac{t^{V}}{V!},

where

\sigma_{V}=\lim_{\sigma\to 0}\frac{{\rm d}^{V-1}}{{\rm d}\sigma^{V-1}}\biggl(\frac{\sigma^{V}}{G(\sigma)^{V}}\biggr).

(E.21)

The proof of this theorem follows almost immediately from the Cauchy integral formula. In our situation, for any fixed
0<\tau<1
,
and
H\in\RR
, we have that

\mathfrak{I}(0;\tau,t,H)=0,\qquad\mathfrak{I}_{\sigma}(0;\tau,t,H)=\frac{1}{3}\bigl(\tau^{2}-1\bigr)\neq 0,

and so we can develop a Taylor series expansion of
\sigma(\tau,t,H)
about
t=0
, by taking
G(\sigma)
to be

G(\sigma):=\mathfrak{I}(\sigma;\tau,0,H)=-\frac{1}{9}\tau^{2}\sigma\bigl(\sigma^{2}-3\bigr)-\frac{1}{3}\frac{\sigma}{(1+\sigma)^{2}}+\frac{2}{3}\biggl(\frac{\sigma}{1-\sigma^{2}}\biggr)^{2}[\cosh H-1].

By the Cauchy integral formula, along with formula (
E.21
), we can write

\sigma_{V}(\tau,H)=\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\oint_{C_{0}}\frac{{\rm d}\zeta}{\mathfrak{I}(\zeta;\tau,0,H)^{V}}=\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\oint_{C_{0}}{\rm e}^{-V\log\mathfrak{I}(\zeta;\tau,0,H)}{\rm d}\zeta,

(E.22)

where
C_{0}
is a sufficiently small positively oriented circle enclosing the origin. We are interested in the large-
V
asymptotics of

\sigma_{V}(\tau,H)
. When
H=0
, and
\tau\neq\frac{1}{4}
, these asymptotics can be calculated explicitly using classical
steepest descent analysis (cf., for example,
[
71
]
). When
{H=0}
, the saddle points of the integrand are

\zeta=1,\qquad-1\pm\tau^{-1/2},\qquad-1\pm{\rm i}\tau^{-1/2}.

The dominant saddle point
\zeta^{*}
is the one nearest to the origin. Thus, we see that

\zeta^{*}(\tau)=\begin{cases}1,&0<\tau<\frac{1}{4},\\
\tau^{-1/2}-1,&\frac{1}{4}<\tau<1.\end{cases}

(Note that this change in the dominant saddle point is ultimately the source of the phase transition). It follows that

\sigma_{V}(\tau,0)=\frac{\pm}{2\pi{\rm i}}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{V[-\log\mathfrak{I}(\zeta^{*};\tau,0,0)]_{\zeta\zeta}}}\biggl[\frac{1}{\mathfrak{I}(\zeta^{*};\tau,0,0)}\biggr]^{V}\bigl[1+\OO\bigl(V^{-1/2}\bigr)\bigr],

where the sign
\pm
is chosen appropriately in accordance with the contour of steepest descent. This addresses the asymptotics of

\sigma_{V}
away from the critical point
\tau=\frac{1}{4}
. In fact, we can actually say more. We summarize the above result, as well as an extension of it to a neighborhood of the critical point, in the following proposition:

Proposition E.8
.

Define
t_{{\rm low}}(\tau):=-\frac{1}{12}+\frac{2}{9}\tau^{2}
,

t_{{\rm high}}(\tau)=-\frac{2}{9}\sqrt{\tau}(\sqrt{\tau}-1)^{2}(\sqrt{\tau}+2)
.
As
V\to\infty
, for any fixed
0<\tau<1
,
\tau\neq\frac{1}{4}
,

\sigma_{V}(\tau,0)=\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{3\pi V}}\sqrt{\dfrac{8\tau^{2}-3}{16\tau^{2}-1}}t_{{\rm low}}(\tau)^{-V}\bigl[1+\OO\bigl(V^{-1/2}\bigr)\bigr],&0<\tau<\frac{1}{4},\\[4.2679pt]
\dfrac{(\sqrt{\tau}-1)}{2\sqrt{3\pi V}}\sqrt{\dfrac{2+\sqrt{\tau}}{\tau(2\sqrt{\tau}-1)}}t_{{\rm high}}(\tau)^{-V}\bigl[1+\OO\bigl(V^{-1/2}\bigr)\bigr],&\frac{1}{4}<\tau<1.\end{cases}

(E.23)

If
\tau
belongs to a sufficiently small neighborhood of
\tau=\frac{1}{4}
, then we have the uniform in
\tau
asymptotic

\displaystyle\sigma_{V}(\tau,0)=[t_{{\rm low}}(\tau)t_{{\rm high}}(\tau)]^{-V/2}\biggl[\frac{a_{0}(\tau)}{V^{1/3}}\Ai(V^{2/3}s(\tau))+\frac{b_{0}(\tau)}{V^{2/3}}\Ai^{\prime}(V^{2/3}s(\tau))\biggr]

\displaystyle\hphantom{\sigma_V(\tau,0) =}{}\times\bigl[1+\OO\bigl(V^{-1/3}\bigr)\bigr],

Where
\Ai(z)
denotes the Airy function,
s(\tau)\geq 0
is the continuous function

s(\tau)=\frac{3}{4}\biggl|\log\biggl[\frac{t_{{\rm low}}(\tau)}{t_{{\rm high}}(\tau)}\biggr]\biggr|^{2/3},

(E.24)

satisfying
s\bigl(\frac{1}{4}\bigr)=0
, and
a_{0}(\tau)
,
b_{0}(\tau)
are given by

\displaystyle a_{0}(\tau)=\frac{s(\tau)^{1/4}}{2}\Biggl[\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\sqrt{\frac{3-8\tau^{2}}{1-16\tau^{2}}}+\sqrt{\frac{(1-\sqrt{\tau})^{2}(2+\sqrt{\tau})}{3\tau(1-2\sqrt{\tau})}}\Biggr],

\displaystyle b_{0}(\tau)=\frac{s(\tau)^{-1/4}}{2}\Biggl[\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\sqrt{\frac{3-8\tau^{2}}{1-16\tau^{2}}}-\sqrt{\frac{(1-\sqrt{\tau})^{2}(2+\sqrt{\tau})}{3\tau(1-2\sqrt{\tau})}}\Biggr].

Proof.

As discussed above, when
\tau\neq\frac{1}{4}
, all of the saddle points are simple, and classical steepest descent analysis
yields the expansion (
E.23
). However, when
\tau\to\frac{1}{4}
, the saddle points
\zeta=1
,

\zeta=-1+\tau^{-1/2}
coalesce, and the expansions in the low and high temperature regimes are no longer valid. Indeed,
upon direct inspection of the expansions (
E.23
), one sees that the leading terms in
both expressions diverge as
\tau\to\frac{1}{4}
. In principle, one can find an asymptotic expansion which holds precisely at

\tau=\frac{1}{4}
. However, we can find a more robust expansion, which holds uniformly for
\tau
sufficiently close to

\frac{1}{4}
; this kind of asymptotic expansion was first demonstrated in
[
25
]
. The proof relies on the construction
of a family of conformal maps
\zeta\mapsto u(\zeta;\tau)
, which satisfy the algebraic equation

-\log\mathfrak{I}(\zeta;\tau,0,0)=\frac{1}{3}u^{3}-s(\tau)u+C(\tau).

(E.25)

Here
s
,
C
are determined by the equations

\displaystyle-\log\mathfrak{I}(1;\tau,0,0)=-\frac{2}{3}s(\tau)^{3/2}+C(\tau),

\displaystyle-\log\mathfrak{I}\bigl(-1+\tau^{-1/2};\tau,0,0\bigr)=+\frac{2}{3}s(\tau)^{3/2}+C(\tau).

(
note that these conditions guarantee that
\frac{{\rm d}u}{{\rm d}\zeta}\neq 0,\infty
)
. One finds that

\displaystyle C(\tau)=-\frac{1}{2}\log[t_{{\rm low}}(\tau)t_{{\rm high}}(\tau)],

\displaystyle s(\tau)=\biggl((-1)^{\mathbf{1}_{\{\tau>\frac{1}{4}\}}}\frac{3}{4}\log\biggl[\frac{t_{{\rm low}}(\tau)}{t_{{\rm high}}(\tau)}\biggr]\biggr)^{2/3}.

(
Note that
\frac{t_{{\rm low}}}{t_{{\rm high}}}>1
for
0<\tau<\frac{1}{4}
, and is smaller than
1
for
\frac{1}{4}<\tau<1
, and
so the above expression for
S
is equivalent to (
E.24
)
)
. Provided that
\tau
is sufficiently close to
\frac{1}{4}
,
C(\tau)
,
s(\tau)
are positive and real-valued.
Following
[
25
]
, we can then rewrite the integral (
E.22
) as

\sigma_{V}(\tau,0)=\frac{{\rm e}^{VC(\tau)}}{2\pi{\rm i}}\oint_{\tilde{C}}{\rm e}^{V\left[\frac{1}{3}u^{3}-s(\tau)u\right]}\frac{{\rm d}\zeta}{{\rm d}u}{\rm d}u,

where
\tilde{C}
is the image of the circle
C
under the conformal map
u(\zeta)
. Now, we can express
\frac{{\rm d}\zeta}{{\rm d}u}
as

\frac{{\rm d}\zeta}{{\rm d}u}=\sum a_{k}(\tau)\bigl(u^{2}-s(\tau)\bigr)^{k}+\sum b_{k}(\tau)u\bigl(u^{2}-s(\tau)\bigr)^{k};

the only terms we calculate explicitly are
a_{0}(\tau)
,
b_{0}(\tau)
, as they will be the dominant terms in the expansion. Differentiating (
E.25
), and expanding using L’Hôpital’s rule, one can determine that

\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{{\rm d}\zeta}{{\rm d}u}\bigg|_{u=s^{1/2}}\biggr)^{2}=\frac{4}{3}\frac{3-8\tau^{2}}{1-16\tau^{2}}s(\tau)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{{\rm d}\zeta}{{\rm d}u}\bigg|_{u=-s^{1/2}}\biggr)^{2}=\frac{(1-\sqrt{\tau})^{2}(2+\sqrt{\tau})}{3\tau(1-2\sqrt{\tau})}s(\tau)^{1/2},

and so these derivatives are determined up to a sign, which can be determined for instance by requiring the asymptotic expansion we are developing match with classical steepest descent results precisely at
\tau=\frac{1}{4}
. This determines
a_{0}(\tau)
,
b_{0}(\tau)

to be

\displaystyle a_{0}(\tau)=\frac{s(\tau)^{1/4}}{2}\Biggl[\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\sqrt{\frac{3-8\tau^{2}}{1-16\tau^{2}}}+\sqrt{\frac{(1-\sqrt{\tau})^{2}(2+\sqrt{\tau})}{3\tau(1-2\sqrt{\tau})}}\Biggr],

\displaystyle b_{0}(\tau)=\frac{s(\tau)^{-1/4}}{2}\Biggl[\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\sqrt{\frac{3-8\tau^{2}}{1-16\tau^{2}}}-\sqrt{\frac{(1-\sqrt{\tau})^{2}(2+\sqrt{\tau})}{3\tau(1-2\sqrt{\tau})}}\Biggr].

These functions are indeed continuous, as one can readily compute the left/right limits as
\tau\to\frac{1}{4}
.
One finally obtains that

\displaystyle\sigma_{V}(\tau,0)

\displaystyle\!=\!{\rm e}^{VC(\tau)}\!\biggl[\frac{a_{0}(\tau)}{V^{1/3}}\Ai\bigl(V^{2/3}s(\tau)\bigr)+\frac{b_{0}(\tau)}{V^{2/3}}\Ai^{\prime}\bigl(V^{2/3}s(\tau)\bigr)\!\biggr]\!\bigl[1+\OO\bigl(V^{-1/3}\bigr)\bigr]

\displaystyle\!=\![t_{{\rm low}}(\tau)t_{{\rm high}}(\tau)]^{-V/2}\!\biggl[\frac{a_{0}(\tau)}{V^{1/3}}\Ai\bigl(V^{2/3}s(\tau)\bigr)+\frac{b_{0}(\tau)}{V^{2/3}}\Ai^{\prime}\bigl(V^{2/3}s(\tau)\bigr)\!\biggr]\!\bigl[1+\OO\bigl(V^{-1/3}\bigr)\bigr],

as desired. As a consistency check, for
\tau\neq\frac{1}{4}
, if one further replaces
\Ai(V^{2/3}s(\tau))
with its asymptotic expansion for large
V
(note that
s(\tau)\geq 0
, by construction), one can match the formulae (
E.23
).
∎

Remark E.9
.

We can also extend the some of the above calculations by continuity to the case of nonzero
H
, provided
H
is sufficiently small. However, in a neighborhood of the critical point
\tau=\frac{1}{4}
,
H=0
, the above calculations do not apply, as we shall now explain.

Provided that
\tau\neq\frac{1}{4}
, one can continue the dominant saddle point
\zeta^{*}
to nonzero
H
in an unambiguous manner, and the dominant saddle remains simple for sufficiently small
H
. For
{\frac{1}{4}<\tau<1}
,
recall that the dominant saddle point was
\zeta^{*}=-1+\tau^{-1/2}
. In this case,
\zeta^{*}
is an analytic function of
H
in a neighborhood of
H=0
, and admits the expansion

\zeta^{*}(\tau,H)=-1+\tau^{-1/2}-\frac{(1-\sqrt{\tau})(1+2\sqrt{\tau}+2\tau)}{2(1-2\sqrt{\tau})^{4}}H^{2}+\OO\bigl(H^{4}\bigr).

By continuity, this is the nearest saddle point to the origin when
H
is sufficiently small, for any fixed
\frac{1}{4}<\tau<1
.
The situation is slightly more complicated when
0<\tau<\frac{1}{4}
. In this case, one sees that the saddle point at
\zeta^{*}=1
is actually the result of
4
simple saddle points for nonzero
H
. These saddle points do
not
depend analytically on
H
in a neighborhood of
H=0
, and instead have a series expansion in powers of
|H|^{1/2}
. One finds that

\zeta=1\pm\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-16\tau^{2})^{1/4}}|H|^{1/2}+\OO(|H|),\qquad 1\pm\frac{{\rm i}\sqrt{2}}{(1-16\tau^{2})^{1/4}}|H|^{1/2}+\OO(|H|).

These saddle points are continuous in
H
; the one nearest to the origin for
|H|>0
is therefore

\zeta^{*}=1-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{(1-16\tau^{2})^{1/4}}|H|^{1/2}+\OO(|H|).

When
H\neq 0
, it is apparent that the saddle point at
\zeta=1
split into a quadruple of saddle points when
H\neq 0
,
and for
0<\tau<\frac{1}{4}
. This is also the case when
\frac{1}{4}<\tau<1
, although the saddle point is irrelevant for the
calculations at hand. However, when
\tau=\frac{1}{4}
, the saddle points at
\zeta=1
,
\zeta=-1+\tau^{-1/2}
merge; when the
external field
H
is “turned on” at this point, one finds that the saddle point at
\zeta=1
splits into
five
distinct
saddle points, each which has the expansion

\zeta_{j}=1-2^{1/5}\mathfrak{s}^{j}|H|^{2/5}+\OO\bigl(|H|^{4/5}\bigr),\qquad j=0,\dots,4,

where
\mathfrak{s}:={\rm e}^{\frac{2\pi{\rm i}}{5}}
is a
5^{\rm th}
root of unity. If one is interested in asymptotics precisely at
\tau=\frac{1}{4}
(as one might be, if attempting to calculate the critical exponent
\delta
from the Landau theory, cf.
[
14
]
), then one
recognizes
\zeta_{0}
as the dominant saddle point, and can proceed to use classical steepest descent to calculate the asymptotics of

\sigma_{V}\bigl(\frac{1}{4},H\bigr)
there. A more interesting calculation, which is for the moment out of reach, would be to obtain
uniform

asymptotics in a neighborhood of the critical point
\tau=\frac{1}{4}
,
H=0
, as we have done in the case when we ignored the parameter
H
.
This would require an analysis similar to that of
[
25
]
, but instead for a merging of
5
branch points simultaneously. Similarly to
how the uniform asymptotics arising from the merging of
two
branch points was written in terms of solutions to a
second order
equation (the Airy equation), one should expect that the asymptotics arising from
5
merging branch points should be characterized in terms of a degree
5
equation, whose solutions are integrals of the form

P(x,a,b,c;\gamma):=\frac{1}{2\pi{\rm i}}\int_{\gamma}{\rm e}^{\frac{1}{6}\zeta^{6}+a\zeta^{4}+b\zeta^{3}+c\zeta^{2}-x\zeta}{\rm d}\zeta,

where the parameters
a
,
b
,
c
,
x
are functions of
\tau
,
H
, chosen so that all tend to
0
as
\tau\to\frac{1}{4}
,
H\to 0
. One must construct an associated conformal map, which is by no means a simple task. We hope to pursue this analysis in a later work.
